Theory & Documentation

This section documents the mechanics and processing logic implemented by the application. The analysis represents the bridge as a one-dimensional, linearly elastic Euler-Bernoulli beam system assembled by the direct stiffness method. Results apply only to the idealized model and should be independently verified before design use.

Part 1: Engineering Theory

1. Structural idealization and degrees of freedom

Each span is one prismatic beam element. Plane sections are assumed to remain plane; transverse shear deformation, geometric nonlinearity, material nonlinearity, and torsion are excluded. Each node has one vertical translation and one rotation:

\[\mathbf d_e=\begin{bmatrix}v_i&\theta_i&v_j&\theta_j\end{bmatrix}^{\mathsf T}\]Equation 1

where \(\mathbf d_e\) is the element displacement vector, \(v_i\) and \(v_j\) are the vertical translations at the element's left and right nodes, and \(\theta_i\) and \(\theta_j\) are the corresponding nodal rotations.

2. Euler-Bernoulli flexure

\[M(x)=EI\,\frac{\mathrm d^2v(x)}{\mathrm dx^2}\]Equation 2

where \(M(x)\) is bending moment at position \(x\), \(E\) is Young's modulus, \(I\) is the second moment of area, and \(v(x)\) is transverse displacement. The program accepts relative \(EI\), so stiffness distribution is governed by the entered ratios when all spans share a common scale.

3. Element stiffness matrix

\[\mathbf k_e=\frac{EI}{L^3}\begin{bmatrix}12&6L&-12&6L\\6L&4L^2&-6L&2L^2\\-12&-6L&12&-6L\\6L&2L^2&-6L&4L^2\end{bmatrix}\]Equation 3

where \(\mathbf k_e\) is the local element stiffness matrix, \(EI\) is flexural rigidity, and \(L\) is element length. The program adds each coefficient to the associated positions in the global stiffness matrix.

4. Consistent nodal loading

A full-span uniformly distributed load is converted to the following equivalent nodal vector:

\[\mathbf f_{e,q}=\begin{bmatrix}qL/2&qL^2/12&qL/2&-qL^2/12\end{bmatrix}^{\mathsf T}\]Equation 4

where \(\mathbf f_{e,q}\) is the element load vector, \(q\) is uniform load intensity, and \(L\) is element length.

An axle within an element is distributed to its nodes using cubic Hermitian shape functions:

\[\xi=\frac{a}{L},\qquad\begin{aligned}N_1&=1-3\xi^2+2\xi^3,&N_2&=L(\xi-2\xi^2+\xi^3),\\N_3&=3\xi^2-2\xi^3,&N_4&=L(-\xi^2+\xi^3)\end{aligned}\]Equation 5

where \(\xi\) is the dimensionless local coordinate, \(a\) is the axle distance from the element's left node, \(L\) is element length, and \(N_1\) through \(N_4\) are the beam interpolation functions.

\[\mathbf f_{e,P}=P\begin{bmatrix}N_1&N_2&N_3&N_4\end{bmatrix}^{\mathsf T}\]Equation 6

where \(\mathbf f_{e,P}\) is the consistent element load vector for one axle, \(P\) is axle load, and \(N_1\) through \(N_4\) are defined by Equation 5. All active axle and distributed-load vectors are combined by linear superposition.

5. Global solution and support reactions

\[\mathbf K\mathbf D=\mathbf F\]Equation 7

where \(\mathbf K\) is the assembled global stiffness matrix, \(\mathbf D\) is the global displacement vector, and \(\mathbf F\) is the assembled nodal load vector. Pin supports restrain vertical translation, fixed supports restrain translation and rotation, and free nodes restrain neither. The reduced free-degree system is solved by Gaussian elimination.

\[\mathbf R=\mathbf K\mathbf D-\mathbf F\]Equation 8

where \(\mathbf R\) is the global residual vector containing restrained-degree reactions, while \(\mathbf K\), \(\mathbf D\), and \(\mathbf F\) retain the definitions given for Equation 7. Reported reaction signs follow the application's display convention.

6. Shear and moment recovery

Element end forces are recovered from the stiffness response minus the element's consistent load vector. Internal actions are then evaluated from section equilibrium at 121 equally spaced points per element:

\[V(x)=V_0-qx-\sum_{a_r\le x}P_r\]Equation 9

where \(V(x)\) is shear at local coordinate \(x\), \(V_0\) is recovered left-end shear, \(q\) is uniform load intensity, \(P_r\) is axle load \(r\), and \(a_r\) is that axle's local coordinate.

\[M(x)=-M_0+V_0x-\frac{qx^2}{2}-\sum_{a_r\le x}P_r(x-a_r)\]Equation 10

where \(M(x)\) is bending moment at local coordinate \(x\), \(M_0\) is recovered left-end element moment, \(V_0\) is recovered left-end shear, \(q\) is uniform load intensity, \(P_r\) is axle load \(r\), and \(a_r\) is its local coordinate. Linear interpolation maps the recovered values to table stations.

7. Moving-load path and envelopes

\[x_r(p)=p-s_r\]Equation 11

where \(x_r(p)\) is the global bridge position of axle \(r\) at vehicle-position parameter \(p\), and \(s_r\) is that axle's offset from Axle 1. An axle is active only when \(0\le x_r(p)\le L_{\mathrm{bridge}}\), where \(L_{\mathrm{bridge}}\) is total bridge length.

\[p_n=p_0+n\Delta p\]Equation 12

where \(p_n\) is the vehicle position for load case \(n\), \(p_0\) is the initial position, \(\Delta p\) is the user-entered nudge distance, and \(n\) is a nonnegative integer. The script appends an exact final position so the last axle reaches the right end.

\[E_{\max}(x_j)=\max_n E_n(x_j),\qquad E_{\min}(x_j)=\min_n E_n(x_j)\]Equation 13

where \(E_n(x_j)\) is a response in case \(n\) at reporting station \(x_j\), and \(E_{\max}(x_j)\) and \(E_{\min}(x_j)\) are its upper and lower envelopes. Equation 13 is applied independently to moment, shear, and support reactions.

8. Numerical scope and verification

  • The nudge distance governs the moving-load search resolution. Critical results should be checked for convergence using smaller values of \(\Delta p\).
  • Span divisions define results-table stations, while internal-force recovery uses 120 intervals per span.
  • The analysis does not automatically apply load combinations, load factors, dynamic allowance, lane distribution, resistance factors, section-capacity equations, second-order effects, or nonlinear response.
  • Continuity and support conditions must represent a stable structure. A singular reduced stiffness matrix triggers an error.
Part 2: Coding Theory

The program is a standalone HTML application. CSS controls the screen and report layouts; JavaScript stores the model, performs matrix calculations, generates SVG diagrams and tables, exports CSV data, and saves a new working HTML file.

  1. State and inputs: arrays hold axles, spans, and supports. Each editable control updates the associated object and reruns the model.
  2. Geometry: cumulative span lengths generate global node coordinates and output stations.
  3. Moving-load loop: Equation 12 generates vehicle positions. Equation 11 maps each axle to a global coordinate, after which active axles are assigned to a span and local coordinate.
  4. Assembly: the script creates each element matrix from Equation 3 and load vectors from Equations 4 and 6, then assembles them into the global system in Equation 7.
  5. Boundary conditions and solver: restrained and free degrees of freedom are separated. Gaussian elimination solves the reduced system, with a singularity check for unstable models.
  6. Post-processing: Equation 8 yields reactions. Equations 9 and 10 recover shear and moment. Linear interpolation evaluates responses at reporting stations.
  7. Envelope storage: each case updates the maximum and minimum response arrays according to Equation 13 and records the case governing maximum moment.
  8. Rendering and export: the output object feeds the summary cards, tables, beam preview, SVG envelope diagrams, iteration log, and CSV export.
  9. PDF report: Save PDF Report reruns the model, exposes all required report panels, waits for MathJax startup and typesetting to complete, allows two browser paint frames plus a short delay, and then opens the browser print dialog.
  10. Persistence: Save Working File clones the complete document, including this marked theory section, MathJax configuration, styles, equations, and print logic, and replaces only the saved input-state constant. Future source updates should retain all blocks marked PERSISTENT THEORY.
Part 3: References
